From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia
Study of DNA modifications that do not change its sequence
For other uses, see [Epigenetic (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetic_\(disambiguation\) "Epigenetic \(disambiguation\)").
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Epigenetic_mechanisms.png/250px-Epigenetic_mechanisms.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Epigenetic_mechanisms.png)Epigenetic mechanisms
**Epigenetics** is the study of changes in [gene expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_expression "Gene expression") that occur without altering the [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA "DNA") sequence.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Epigenetics_2009_review-1) The Greek prefix _epi-_ (ἐπι- "over, outside of, around") in _epigenetics_ implies features that are "on top of" or "in addition to" the traditional DNA-sequence-based mechanism of inheritance.[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-science-2) Epigenetics usually involves changes that persist through cell division, and affect the regulation of gene expression.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-3) Such effects on cellular and physiological traits may result from environmental factors, or be part of normal development. 
The term also refers to the mechanism behind these changes: functionally relevant alterations to the [genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genome "Genome") that do not involve mutations in the [nucleotide sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleotide_sequence "Nucleotide sequence"). Examples of mechanisms that produce such changes are [DNA methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation "DNA methylation") and [histone modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_modification "Histone modification"), each of which alters how genes are expressed without altering the underlying [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA "DNA") sequence.[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-4) Further, non-coding RNA sequences have been shown to play a key role in the regulation of gene expression.[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-5) Gene expression can be controlled through the action of [repressor proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Repressor_protein "Repressor protein") that attach to [silencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silencer_\(DNA\) "Silencer \(DNA\)") regions of the DNA. These epigenetic changes may last through [cell divisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_division "Cell division") for the duration of the cell's life, and may also last for multiple generations, even though they do not involve changes in the underlying DNA sequence of the organism;[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17522671-6) instead, non-genetic factors cause the organism's genes to behave (or "express themselves") differently.[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-7)
One example of an epigenetic change in [eukaryotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eukaryotic "Eukaryotic") biology is the process of [cellular differentiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellular_differentiation "Cellular differentiation"). During [morphogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphogenesis "Morphogenesis"), [totipotent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totipotent "Totipotent") [stem cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_cells "Stem cells") become the various [pluripotent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluripotent "Pluripotent") [cell lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_line "Cell line") of the [embryo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryo "Embryo"), which in turn become fully differentiated cells. In other words, as a single fertilized [egg cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_cell "Egg cell") – the [zygote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zygote "Zygote") – continues to [divide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitosis "Mitosis"), the resulting daughter cells develop into the different cell types in an organism, including [neurons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurons "Neurons"), [muscle cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscle_cells "Muscle cells"), [epithelium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epithelium "Epithelium"), [endothelium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endothelium "Endothelium") of [blood vessels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_vessels "Blood vessels"), etc., by activating some genes while inhibiting the expression of others.[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17522676-8)
## Definitions
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Definitions")]
The term _epigenesis_ has a generic meaning of "extra growth" that has been used in English since the 17th century.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-9) In scientific publications, the term _epigenetics_ started to appear in the 1930s (see Fig. on the right). However, its contemporary meaning emerged only in the 1990s.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Moore_2015-10)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/EpigenByYear_1.png/250px-EpigenByYear_1.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:EpigenByYear_1.png)Number of patent families and non-patent documents with the term "epigenetic*" by publication year
A definition of the concept of _epigenetic trait_ as a "stably heritable phenotype resulting from changes in a chromosome without alterations in the DNA sequence" was formulated at a [Cold Spring Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_Spring_Harbor_Laboratory "Cold Spring Harbor Laboratory") meeting in 2008,[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19339683-11) although alternate definitions that include non-heritable traits are still being used widely.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-NIH-12)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Waddington's canalisation, 1940s")]
The hypothesis of epigenetic changes affecting the expression of [chromosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromosome "Chromosome") was put forth by the Russian biologist [Nikolai Koltsov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Koltsov "Nikolai Koltsov").[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-13) From the generic meaning, and the associated adjective _epigenetic_ , British embryologist [C. H. Waddington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._H._Waddington "C. H. Waddington") coined the term _epigenetics_ in 1942 as pertaining to _[epigenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenesis_\(biology\) "Epigenesis \(biology\)")_ , in parallel to [Valentin Haecker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valentin_Haecker "Valentin Haecker")'s 'phenogenetics' (_Phänogenetik_).[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-waddington-14) _Epigenesis_ in the context of the biology of that period referred to the [differentiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cellular_differentiation "Cellular differentiation") of cells from their initial [totipotent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totipotent "Totipotent") state during [embryonic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryonic_development "Embryonic development").[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-15)
When Waddington coined the term, the physical nature of [genes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene "Gene") and their role in heredity was not known. He used it instead as a conceptual model of how genetic components might interact with their surroundings to produce a [phenotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype "Phenotype"); he used the phrase "[epigenetic landscape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetic_landscape "Epigenetic landscape")" as a metaphor for [biological development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphogenesis "Morphogenesis"). Waddington held that cell fates were established during development in a process he called [canalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canalisation_\(genetics\) "Canalisation \(genetics\)") much as a marble rolls down to the point of [lowest local elevation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_optimum "Local optimum").[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Waddington2014-16) Waddington suggested visualising increasing irreversibility of cell type differentiation as ridges rising between the valleys where the marbles (analogous to cells) are travelling.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-17)
In recent times, Waddington's notion of the epigenetic landscape has been rigorously formalized in the context of the [systems dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/System_dynamics "System dynamics") state approach to the study of cell-fate.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-18)[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-sciencedirect.com-19) Cell-fate determination is predicted to exhibit certain dynamics, such as attractor-convergence (the attractor can be an equilibrium point, limit cycle or [strange attractor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strange_attractor "Strange attractor")) or oscillatory.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-sciencedirect.com-19)
### Contemporary
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Contemporary")]
In 1990, [Robin Holliday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Holliday "Robin Holliday") defined epigenetics as "the study of the mechanisms of temporal and spatial control of gene activity during the development of complex organisms."[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid2265224-20)
More recent usage of the word in biology follows stricter definitions. As defined by [Arthur Riggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Riggs_\(geneticist\) "Arthur Riggs \(geneticist\)") and colleagues, it is "the study of [mitotically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitosis "Mitosis") and/or [meiotically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiosis "Meiosis") heritable changes in gene function that cannot be explained by changes in DNA sequence."[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-isbn0-87969-490-4-21)
The term has also been used, however, to describe processes which have not been demonstrated to be heritable, such as some forms of histone modification. Consequently, there are attempts to redefine "epigenetics" in broader terms that would avoid the constraints of requiring [heritability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heritability "Heritability"). For example, [Adrian Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Peter_Bird "Adrian Peter Bird") defined epigenetics as "the structural adaptation of chromosomal regions so as to register, signal or perpetuate altered activity states."[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17522671-6) This definition would be inclusive of transient modifications associated with [DNA repair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_repair "DNA repair") or [cell-cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell-cycle "Cell-cycle") phases as well as stable changes maintained across multiple cell generations, but exclude others such as templating of membrane architecture and [prions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prions "Prions") unless they impinge on chromosome function. Such redefinitions however are not universally accepted and are still subject to debate.[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-nature2008-22) The [NIH](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health "National Institutes of Health") "Roadmap Epigenomics Project", which ran from 2008 to 2017, uses the following definition: "For purposes of this program, epigenetics refers to both heritable changes in gene activity and [expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_expression "Gene expression") (in the progeny of cells or of individuals) and also stable, long-term alterations in the transcriptional potential of a cell that are not necessarily heritable."[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-23) In 2008, a consensus definition of the epigenetic trait, a "stably heritable phenotype resulting from changes in a chromosome without alterations in the DNA sequence," was made at a [Cold Spring Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_Spring_Harbor_Laboratory "Cold Spring Harbor Laboratory") meeting.[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19339683-11)
The similarity of the word to "genetics" has generated many parallel usages. The "[epigenome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenome "Epigenome")" is a parallel to the word "[genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genome "Genome")", referring to the overall epigenetic state of a cell, and [epigenomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenomics "Epigenomics") refers to global analyses of epigenetic changes across the entire genome.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-NIH-12) The phrase "[genetic code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_code "Genetic code")" has also been adapted – the "[epigenetic code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetic_code "Epigenetic code")" has been used to describe the set of epigenetic features that create different phenotypes in different cells from the same underlying DNA sequence. Taken to its extreme, the "epigenetic code" could represent the total state of the cell, with the position of each molecule accounted for in an _epigenomic map_ , a diagrammatic representation of the gene expression, DNA methylation and histone modification status of a particular genomic region. More typically, the term is used in reference to systematic efforts to measure specific, relevant forms of epigenetic information such as the [histone code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_code_hypothesis "Histone code hypothesis") or [DNA methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation "DNA methylation") patterns.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
## Mechanisms
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Mechanisms")]
[Covalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covalent "Covalent") modification of either DNA (e.g. cytosine methylation and hydroxymethylation) or of histone proteins (e.g. lysine acetylation, lysine and arginine methylation, serine and threonine phosphorylation, and lysine ubiquitination and sumoylation) play central roles in many types of epigenetic inheritance. Therefore, the word "epigenetics" is sometimes used as a synonym for these processes. However, this can be misleading. Chromatin remodeling is not always inherited, and not all epigenetic inheritance involves chromatin remodeling.[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17407749-24) In 2019, a further lysine modification appeared in the scientific literature linking epigenetics modification to cell metabolism, i.e. lactylation.[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-25)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Nucleosome_1KX5_2.png/250px-Nucleosome_1KX5_2.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nucleosome_1KX5_2.png)DNA associates with histone proteins to form chromatin.
Because the [phenotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype "Phenotype") of a cell or individual is affected by which of its genes are transcribed, heritable [transcription states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcription_\(genetics\) "Transcription \(genetics\)") can give rise to epigenetic effects. There are several layers of regulation of [gene expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_expression "Gene expression"). One way that genes are regulated is through the remodeling of chromatin. Chromatin is the complex of DNA and the [histone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone "Histone") proteins with which it associates. If the way that DNA is wrapped around the histones changes, gene expression can change as well. Chromatin remodeling is accomplished through two main mechanisms: 
  1. The first way is [post translational modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post_translational_modification "Post translational modification") of the amino acids that make up histone proteins. Histone proteins are made up of long chains of amino acids. If the amino acids that are in the chain are changed, the shape of the histone might be modified. DNA is not completely unwound during replication. It is possible, then, that the modified histones may be carried into each new copy of the DNA. Once there, these histones may act as templates, initiating the surrounding new histones to be shaped in the new manner. By altering the shape of the histones around them, these modified histones would ensure that a lineage-specific transcription program is maintained after cell division.
  2. The second way is the addition of methyl groups to the DNA, mostly at [CpG sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CpG_site "CpG site"), to convert [cytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytosine "Cytosine") to [5-methylcytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5-methylcytosine "5-methylcytosine"). 5-Methylcytosine performs much like a regular cytosine, pairing with a guanine in double-stranded DNA. However, when methylated cytosines are present in CpG sites in the [promoter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promoter_\(genetics\) "Promoter \(genetics\)") and [enhancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhancer_\(genetics\) "Enhancer \(genetics\)") regions of genes, the genes are often repressed.[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid30619465-26)[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid31399642-27) When methylated cytosines are present in CpG sites in the gene body (in the [coding region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coding_region "Coding region") excluding the transcription start site) expression of the gene is often enhanced. Transcription of a gene usually depends on a [transcription factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcription_factor "Transcription factor") binding to a (10 base or less) [recognition sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recognition_sequence "Recognition sequence") at the enhancer that interacts with the promoter region of that gene ([Gene expression#Enhancers, transcription factors, mediator complex and DNA loops in mammalian transcription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_expression#Enhancers,_transcription_factors,_mediator_complex_and_DNA_loops_in_mammalian_transcription "Gene expression")).[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid22868264-28) About 22% of transcription factors are inhibited from binding when the recognition sequence has a methylated cytosine. In addition, presence of methylated cytosines at a promoter region can attract [methyl-CpG-binding domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methyl-CpG-binding_domain "Methyl-CpG-binding domain") (MBD) proteins. All MBDs interact with [nucleosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome") remodeling and [histone deacetylase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_deacetylase "Histone deacetylase") complexes, which leads to gene silencing. In addition, another covalent modification involving methylated cytosine is its [demethylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_demethylation "DNA demethylation") by [TET enzymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TET_enzymes "TET enzymes"). Hundreds of such demethylations occur, for instance, during [learning and memory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_demethylation#Learnking_and_memory "DNA demethylation") forming events in [neurons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuron "Neuron").[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid28620075-29)[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Bernstein-30)


There is frequently a reciprocal relationship between DNA methylation and histone lysine methylation.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Rose-31) For instance, the [methyl binding domain protein MBD1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methyl-CpG-binding_domain "Methyl-CpG-binding domain"), attracted to and associating with [methylated cytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5-Methylcytosine "5-Methylcytosine") in a DNA CpG site, can also associate with H3K9 [methyltransferase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methyltransferase "DNA methyltransferase") activity to methylate histone 3 at lysine 9. On the other hand, DNA maintenance methylation by [DNMT1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNMT1 "DNMT1") appears to partly rely on recognition of histone methylation on the nucleosome present at the DNA site to carry out cytosine methylation on newly synthesized DNA.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Rose-31) There is further crosstalk between DNA methylation carried out by [DNMT3A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNMT3A "DNMT3A") and [DNMT3B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNMT3B "DNMT3B") and histone methylation so that there is a correlation between the genome-wide distribution of DNA methylation and histone methylation.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Li2021-32)
Mechanisms of heritability of histone state are not well understood; however, much is known about the mechanism of heritability of DNA methylation state during cell division and differentiation. Heritability of methylation state depends on certain enzymes (such as [DNMT1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methyltransferase "DNA methyltransferase")) that have a higher affinity for 5-methylcytosine than for cytosine. If this enzyme reaches a "hemimethylated" portion of DNA (where 5-methylcytosine is in only one of the two DNA strands) the enzyme will methylate the other half. However, it is now known that DNMT1 physically interacts with the protein [UHRF1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UHRF1 "UHRF1"). UHRF1 has been recently recognized as essential for DNMT1-mediated maintenance of DNA methylation. UHRF1 is the protein that specifically recognizes hemi-methylated DNA, therefore bringing DNMT1 to its substrate to maintain DNA methylation.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Li2021-32)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fc/Histone_tails_set_for_transcriptional_activation.jpg/250px-Histone_tails_set_for_transcriptional_activation.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Histone_tails_set_for_transcriptional_activation.jpg)
Activation
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/Histone_tails_set_for_transcriptional_repression.jpg/250px-Histone_tails_set_for_transcriptional_repression.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Histone_tails_set_for_transcriptional_repression.jpg)
Repression
Some acetylations and some methylations of lysines (symbol K) are activation signals for transcription when present on a [nucleosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome"), as shown in the top figure. Some methylations on lysines or arginine (R) are repression signals for transcription when present on a [nucleosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome"), as shown in the bottom figure. [Nucleosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome") consist of four pairs of [histone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone "Histone") proteins in a tightly assembled core region plus up to 30% of each histone remaining in a loosely organized tail[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid33133421-33) (only one tail of each pair is shown). DNA is wrapped around the histone core proteins in [chromatin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromatin "Chromatin"). The lysines (K) are designated with a number showing their position as, for instance (K4), indicating lysine as the 4th amino acid from the amino (N) end of the tail in the histone protein. [Methylations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methylation "Methylation") [Me], and [acetylations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylation "Acetylation") [Ac] are common [post-translational modifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-translational_modification "Post-translational modification") on the lysines of the histone tails.
Although histone modifications occur throughout the entire sequence, the unstructured N-termini of histones (called histone tails) are particularly highly modified. These modifications include [acetylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylation "Acetylation"), [methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methylation "Methylation"), [ubiquitylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubiquitylation "Ubiquitylation"), [phosphorylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorylation "Phosphorylation"), [sumoylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumoylation "Sumoylation"), ribosylation and citrullination. Acetylation is the most highly studied of these modifications. For example, acetylation of the K14 and K9 [lysines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysine "Lysine") of the tail of histone H3 by histone acetyltransferase enzymes (HATs) is generally related to transcriptional competence[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-34) (see Figure). 
One mode of thinking is that this tendency of acetylation to be associated with "active" transcription is biophysical in nature. Because it normally has a positively charged nitrogen at its end, lysine can bind the negatively charged phosphates of the DNA backbone. The acetylation event converts the positively charged amine group on the side chain into a neutral amide linkage. This removes the positive charge, thus loosening the DNA from the histone. When this occurs, complexes like [SWI/SNF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SWI/SNF "SWI/SNF") and other transcriptional factors can bind to the DNA and allow transcription to occur. This is the "cis" model of the epigenetic function. In other words, changes to the histone tails have a direct effect on the DNA itself.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-35)
Another model of epigenetic function is the "trans" model. In this model, changes to the histone tails act indirectly on the DNA. For example, lysine acetylation may create a binding site for chromatin-modifying enzymes (or transcription machinery as well). This chromatin remodeler can then cause changes to the state of the chromatin. Indeed, a bromodomain – a protein domain that specifically binds acetyl-lysine – is found in many enzymes that help activate transcription, including the SWI/SNF complex. It may be that acetylation acts in this and the previous way to aid in transcriptional activation. 
The idea that modifications act as docking modules for related factors is borne out by [histone methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_methylation "Histone methylation") as well. Methylation of lysine 9 of histone H3 has long been associated with constitutively transcriptionally silent chromatin (constitutive [heterochromatin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterochromatin "Heterochromatin")) (see bottom Figure). It has been determined that a chromodomain (a domain that specifically binds methyl-lysine) in the transcriptionally repressive protein [HP1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterochromatin_Protein_1 "Heterochromatin Protein 1") recruits HP1 to K9 methylated regions. One example that seems to refute this biophysical model for methylation is that tri-methylation of histone H3 at lysine 4 is strongly associated with (and required for full) transcriptional activation (see top Figure). Tri-methylation, in this case, would introduce a fixed positive charge on the tail. 
It has been shown that the histone lysine methyltransferase (KMT) is responsible for this methylation activity in the pattern of histones H3 & H4. This enzyme utilizes a catalytically active site called the SET domain (Suppressor of variegation, Enhancer of Zeste, Trithorax). The SET domain is a 130-amino acid sequence involved in modulating gene activities. This domain has been demonstrated to bind to the histone tail and causes the methylation of the histone.[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid9487389-36)
Differing histone modifications are likely to function in differing ways; acetylation at one position is likely to function differently from acetylation at another position. Also, multiple modifications may occur at the same time, and these modifications may work together to change the behavior of the [nucleosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome"). The idea that multiple dynamic modifications regulate gene transcription in a systematic and reproducible way is called the [histone code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_code "Histone code"), although the idea that histone state can be read linearly as a digital information carrier has been largely debunked. One of the best-understood systems that orchestrate chromatin-based silencing is the [SIR protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SIR_protein "SIR protein") based silencing of the yeast hidden mating-type loci HML and HMR. 
### DNA methylation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: DNA methylation")]
Further information: [Methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methylation "Methylation")
[DNA methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation "DNA methylation") often occurs in repeated sequences, and helps to suppress the expression and movement of '[transposable elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transposable_elements "Transposable elements")':[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-slotkin2007-37) Because [5-methylcytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5-methylcytosine "5-methylcytosine") can spontaneously deaminate to [thymidine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thymidine "Thymidine")(replacing nitrogen by oxygen), CpG sites are frequently mutated and have become rare in the genome, except at [CpG islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CpG_islands "CpG islands") where they typically remain unmethylated. Epigenetic changes of this type thus have the potential to direct increased frequencies of permanent genetic mutation. DNA methylation patterns are known to be established and modified in response to environmental factors by a complex interplay of at least three independent [DNA methyltransferases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methyltransferase "DNA methyltransferase"), DNMT1, DNMT3A, and DNMT3B, the loss of any of which is lethal in mice.[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-li92-38) DNMT1 is the most abundant methyltransferase in somatic cells,[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-robertson99-39) localizes to replication foci,[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-leonhardt92-40) has a 10–40-fold preference for hemimethylated DNA and interacts with the [proliferating cell nuclear antigen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proliferating_cell_nuclear_antigen "Proliferating cell nuclear antigen") (PCNA).[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-chuang97-41)
By preferentially modifying hemimethylated DNA, DNMT1 transfers patterns of methylation to a newly synthesized strand after [DNA replication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_replication "DNA replication"), and therefore is often referred to as the 'maintenance' methyltransferase.[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-robertson00-42) DNMT1 is essential for proper embryonic development, imprinting and X-inactivation.[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-li92-38)[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-li93-43) To emphasize the difference of this molecular mechanism of inheritance from the canonical Watson-Crick base-pairing mechanism of transmission of genetic information, the term 'Epigenetic templating' was introduced.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-44) Furthermore, in addition to the maintenance and transmission of methylated DNA states, the same principle could work in the maintenance and transmission of histone modifications and even cytoplasmic ([structural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_inheritance "Structural inheritance")) heritable states.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid18419815-45)
### RNA methylation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: RNA methylation")]
Further information: [Methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methylation "Methylation")
RNA methylation of N6-methyladenosine (m6A) as the most abundant eukaryotic RNA modification has recently been recognized as an important gene regulatory mechanism.[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid32300195-46)
In 2011, it was demonstrated that the [methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methylation "Methylation") of [mRNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA "Messenger RNA") plays a critical role in human [energy homeostasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_balance_\(biology\) "Energy balance \(biology\)"). The obesity-associated [FTO gene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FTO_gene "FTO gene") is shown to be able to [demethylate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demethylate "Demethylate") [N6-methyladenosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N6-methyladenosine "N6-methyladenosine") in RNA.[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-47)[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-48)
### Histone modifications
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Histone modifications")]
Histones H3 and H4 can also be manipulated through demethylation using [histone lysine demethylase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_lysine_demethylase "Histone lysine demethylase") (KDM). This recently identified enzyme has a catalytically active site called the Jumonji domain (JmjC). The demethylation occurs when JmjC utilizes multiple cofactors to hydroxylate the methyl group, thereby removing it. JmjC is capable of demethylating mono-, di-, and tri-methylated substrates.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19234061-49)
Chromosomal regions can adopt stable and heritable alternative states resulting in bistable gene expression without changes to the DNA sequence. Epigenetic control is often associated with alternative [covalent modifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covalent_modification "Covalent modification") of histones.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Rosenfeld_2009-50) The stability and heritability of states of larger chromosomal regions are suggested to involve positive feedback where modified [nucleosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome") recruit enzymes that similarly modify nearby nucleosomes.[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-51) A simplified stochastic model for this type of epigenetics is found here.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-52)[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17512413-53)
It has been suggested that chromatin-based transcriptional regulation could be mediated by the effect of small RNAs. [Small interfering RNAs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_interfering_RNA "Small interfering RNA") can modulate transcriptional gene expression via epigenetic modulation of targeted [promoters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promoter_\(biology\) "Promoter \(biology\)").[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Morris-54)
### RNA transcripts
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: RNA transcripts")]
Sometimes, a gene, once activated, transcribes a product that directly or indirectly sustains its own activity. For example, [Hnf4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hnf4 "Hnf4") and [MyoD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MyoD "MyoD") enhance the transcription of many liver-specific and muscle-specific genes, respectively, including their own, through the [transcription factor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcription_factor "Transcription factor") activity of the [proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proteins "Proteins") they encode. RNA signalling includes differential recruitment of a hierarchy of generic chromatin modifying complexes and DNA methyltransferases to specific loci by RNAs during differentiation and development.[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19154003-55) Other epigenetic changes are mediated by the production of [different splice forms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_splicing "Alternative splicing") of [RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNA "RNA"), or by formation of double-stranded RNA ([RNAi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RNAi "RNAi")). Descendants of the cell in which the gene was turned on will inherit this activity, even if the original stimulus for gene-activation is no longer present. These genes are often turned on or off by [signal transduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signal_transduction "Signal transduction"), although in some systems where [syncytia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syncytia "Syncytia") or [gap junctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gap_junction "Gap junction") are important, RNA may spread directly to other cells or nuclei by [diffusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffusion "Diffusion"). A large amount of RNA and protein is contributed to the [zygote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zygote "Zygote") by the mother during [oogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oogenesis "Oogenesis") or via [nurse cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nurse_cell "Nurse cell"), resulting in [maternal effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_effect "Maternal effect") phenotypes. A smaller quantity of sperm RNA is transmitted from the father, but there is recent evidence that this epigenetic information can lead to visible changes in several generations of offspring.[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-choi06-56)
#### MicroRNAs
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: MicroRNAs")]
[MicroRNAs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MicroRNA "MicroRNA") (miRNAs) are members of [non-coding RNAs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-coding_RNA "Non-coding RNA") that range in size from 17 to 25 nucleotides. miRNAs regulate a large variety of biological functions in plants and animals.[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Wang-57) So far, in 2013, about 2000 miRNAs have been discovered in humans and these can be found online in a miRNA database.[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-58) Each miRNA expressed in a cell may target about 100 to 200 messenger RNAs(mRNAs) that it downregulates.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-59) Most of the downregulation of mRNAs occurs by causing the decay of the targeted mRNA, while some downregulation occurs at the level of translation into protein.[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-60)
It appears that about 60% of human protein coding genes are regulated by miRNAs.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-61) Many miRNAs are epigenetically regulated. About 50% of miRNA genes are associated with CpG islands,[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Wang-57) that may be repressed by epigenetic methylation. Transcription from methylated CpG islands is strongly and heritably repressed.[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-62) Other miRNAs are epigenetically regulated by either histone modifications or by combined DNA methylation and histone modification.[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Wang-57)
#### sRNAs
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: sRNAs")]
[sRNAs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacterial_small_RNA "Bacterial small RNA") are small (50–250 nucleotides), highly structured, non-coding RNA fragments found in bacteria. They control gene expression including [virulence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virulence "Virulence") genes in pathogens and are viewed as new targets in the fight against drug-resistant bacteria.[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-63) They play an important role in many biological processes, binding to mRNA and protein targets in prokaryotes. Their phylogenetic analyses, for example through sRNA–mRNA target interactions or protein [binding properties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hfq_binding_sRNA "Hfq binding sRNA"), are used to build comprehensive databases.[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-64) sRNA-[gene maps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_map "Gene map") based on their targets in microbial genomes are also constructed.[[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-65)
#### Long non-coding RNAs
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Long non-coding RNAs")]
Numerous investigations have demonstrated the pivotal involvement of long non-coding RNAs (lncRNAs) in the regulation of gene expression and chromosomal modifications, thereby exerting significant control over cellular differentiation. These long non-coding RNAs also contribute to genomic imprinting and the inactivation of the X chromosome.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-66) In invertebrates such as social insects of honey bees, long non-coding RNAs are detected as a possible epigenetic mechanism via allele-specific genes underlying aggression via reciprocal crosses.[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-67)
### Prions
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Prions")]
Further information: [Fungal prions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fungal_prions "Fungal prions")
[Prions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prion "Prion") are [infectious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection "Infection") forms of [proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protein "Protein"). In general, proteins fold into discrete units that perform distinct cellular functions, but some proteins are also capable of forming an infectious conformational state known as a prion. Although often viewed in the context of [infectious disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmissible_spongiform_encephalopathy "Transmissible spongiform encephalopathy"), prions are more loosely defined by their ability to catalytically convert other native state versions of the same protein to an infectious conformational state. It is in this latter sense that they can be viewed as epigenetic agents capable of inducing a phenotypic change without a modification of the genome.[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-68)
[Fungal prions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fungal_prion "Fungal prion") are considered by some to be epigenetic because the infectious phenotype caused by the prion can be inherited without modification of the genome. [PSI+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSI_\(prion\) "PSI \(prion\)") and URE3, discovered in [yeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saccharomyces_cerevisiae "Saccharomyces cerevisiae") in 1965 and 1971, are the two best studied of this type of prion.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-69)[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid5573734-70) Prions can have a phenotypic effect through the sequestration of protein in aggregates, thereby reducing that protein's activity. In PSI+ cells, the loss of the Sup35 protein (which is involved in termination of translation) causes ribosomes to have a higher rate of read-through of stop [codons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codon "Codon"), an effect that results in suppression of [nonsense mutations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonsense_mutation "Nonsense mutation") in other genes.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid225301-71) The ability of Sup35 to form prions may be a conserved trait. It could confer an adaptive advantage by giving cells the ability to [switch into a PSI+ state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_capacitance "Evolutionary capacitance") and express dormant genetic features normally terminated by stop codon mutations.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid11028992-72)[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid15931169-73)[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-74)[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-75)
Prion-based epigenetics has also been observed in _[Saccharomyces cerevisiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saccharomyces_cerevisiae "Saccharomyces cerevisiae")_.[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-76)
## Molecular basis
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Molecular basis")]
Epigenetic changes modify the activation of certain genes, but not the genetic code sequence of DNA.[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Topart-77) The microstructure (not code) of DNA itself or the associated [chromatin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromatin "Chromatin") proteins may be modified, causing activation or silencing. This mechanism enables differentiated cells in a multicellular organism to express only the genes that are necessary for their own activity. Epigenetic changes are preserved when cells divide. Most epigenetic changes only occur within the course of one individual organism's lifetime; however, these epigenetic changes can be transmitted to the organism's offspring through a process called [transgenerational epigenetic inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgenerational_epigenetic_inheritance "Transgenerational epigenetic inheritance"). Moreover, if gene inactivation occurs in a sperm or egg cell that results in fertilization, this epigenetic modification may also be transferred to the next generation.[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17320501-78)
Specific epigenetic processes include [paramutation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramutation "Paramutation"), [bookmarking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bookmarking "Bookmarking"), [imprinting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprinting_\(genetics\) "Imprinting \(genetics\)"), [gene silencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_silencing "Gene silencing"), [X chromosome inactivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-inactivation "X-inactivation"), [position effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Position_effect "Position effect"), [DNA methylation reprogramming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation_reprogramming "DNA methylation reprogramming"), [transvection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transvection_\(genetics\) "Transvection \(genetics\)"), [maternal effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_effect "Maternal effect"), the progress of [carcinogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carcinogenesis "Carcinogenesis"), many effects of [teratogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teratogen "Teratogen"), regulation of [histone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone "Histone") modifications and [heterochromatin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterochromatin "Heterochromatin"), and technical limitations affecting [parthenogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthenogenesis "Parthenogenesis") and [cloning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloning "Cloning").[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-79)[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-80)[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-81)
### DNA damage
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: DNA damage")]
DNA damage can also cause epigenetic changes.[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-82)[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-83)[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-84) DNA damage is very frequent, occurring on average about 60,000 times a day per cell of the human body (see [DNA damage (naturally occurring)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_damage_\(naturally_occurring\) "DNA damage \(naturally occurring\)")). These damages are largely repaired, however, epigenetic changes can still remain at the site of DNA repair.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Cuozzo-85) In particular, a double strand break in DNA can initiate unprogrammed epigenetic gene silencing both by causing DNA methylation as well as by promoting silencing types of histone modifications (chromatin remodeling - see next section).[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-86) In addition, the enzyme [Parp1 (poly(ADP)-ribose polymerase)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poly_ADP_ribose_polymerase "Poly ADP ribose polymerase") and its product poly(ADP)-ribose (PAR) accumulate at sites of DNA damage as part of the repair process.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-87) This accumulation, in turn, directs recruitment and activation of the chromatin remodeling protein, ALC1, that can cause [nucleosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome") remodeling.[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-88) Nucleosome remodeling has been found to cause, for instance, epigenetic silencing of DNA repair gene MLH1.[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-isbn0-87969-490-4-21)[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-89) DNA damaging chemicals, such as [benzene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benzene "Benzene"), [hydroquinone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroquinone "Hydroquinone"), [styrene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Styrene "Styrene"), [carbon tetrachloride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_tetrachloride "Carbon tetrachloride") and [trichloroethylene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trichloroethylene "Trichloroethylene"), cause considerable hypomethylation of DNA, some through the activation of [oxidative stress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxidative_stress "Oxidative stress") pathways.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-90)
Foods are known to alter the epigenetics of rats on different diets.[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-91) Some food components epigenetically increase the levels of DNA repair enzymes such as [MGMT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O-6-methylguanine-DNA_methyltransferase "O-6-methylguanine-DNA methyltransferase") and [MLH1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLH1 "MLH1")[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-92) and [p53](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/P53 "P53").[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-93) Other food components can reduce DNA damage, such as soy [isoflavones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isoflavones "Isoflavones"). In one study, markers for oxidative stress, such as modified nucleotides that can result from DNA damage, were decreased by a 3-week diet supplemented with soy.[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-94) A decrease in oxidative DNA damage was also observed 2 h after consumption of [anthocyanin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthocyanin "Anthocyanin")-rich [bilberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilberry "Bilberry") (_[Vaccinium myrtillius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccinium_myrtillus "Vaccinium myrtillus")_ L.) [pomace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pomace "Pomace") extract.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-95)
### DNA repair
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section: DNA repair")]
Damage to DNA is very common and is constantly being repaired. Epigenetic alterations can accompany DNA repair of oxidative damage or double-strand breaks. In human cells, oxidative DNA damage occurs about 10,000 times a day and DNA double-strand breaks occur about 10 to 50 times a cell cycle in somatic replicating cells (see [DNA damage (naturally occurring)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_damage_\(naturally_occurring\) "DNA damage \(naturally occurring\)")). The selective advantage of DNA repair is to allow the cell to survive in the face of DNA damage. The selective advantage of epigenetic alterations that occur with DNA repair is not clear.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
#### Repair of oxidative DNA damage can alter epigenetic markers
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section: Repair of oxidative DNA damage can alter epigenetic markers")]
In the steady state (with endogenous damages occurring and being repaired), there are about 2,400 oxidatively damaged guanines that form [8-oxo-2'-deoxyguanosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8-oxo-2%27-deoxyguanosine "8-oxo-2'-deoxyguanosine") (8-OHdG) in the average mammalian cell DNA.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid21163908-96) 8-OHdG constitutes about 5% of the oxidative damages commonly present in DNA.[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hamilton-97) The oxidized guanines do not occur randomly among all guanines in DNA. There is a sequence preference for the guanine at a [methylated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation "DNA methylation") CpG site (a cytosine followed by guanine along its [5' → 3' direction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directionality_\(molecular_biology\) "Directionality \(molecular biology\)") and where the cytosine is methylated (5-mCpG)).[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid24571128-98) A 5-mCpG site has the lowest ionization potential for guanine oxidation.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/Initiation_of_DNA_demethylation_at_a_CpG_site.svg/250px-Initiation_of_DNA_demethylation_at_a_CpG_site.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Initiation_of_DNA_demethylation_at_a_CpG_site.svg)Initiation of [DNA demethylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_demethylation "DNA demethylation") at a [CpG site](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CpG_site "CpG site"). In adult somatic cells DNA methylation typically occurs in the context of CpG dinucleotides ([CpG sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CpG_sites "CpG sites")), forming [5-methylcytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5-methylcytosine "5-methylcytosine")-pG, or 5mCpG. Reactive oxygen species (ROS) may attack guanine at the dinucleotide site, forming [8-hydroxy-2'-deoxyguanosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8-oxo-2%27-deoxyguanosine "8-oxo-2'-deoxyguanosine") (8-OHdG), and resulting in a 5mCp-8-OHdG dinucleotide site. The [base excision repair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_excision_repair "Base excision repair") enzyme [OGG1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxoguanine_glycosylase "Oxoguanine glycosylase") targets 8-OHdG and binds to the lesion without immediate excision. OGG1, present at a 5mCp-8-OHdG site recruits [TET1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tet_methylcytosine_dioxygenase_1 "Tet methylcytosine dioxygenase 1") and TET1 oxidizes the 5mC adjacent to the 8-OHdG. This initiates demethylation of 5mC.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Zhou-99)
Oxidized guanine has mispairing potential and is mutagenic.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid31993111-100) [Oxoguanine glycosylase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxoguanine_glycosylase "Oxoguanine glycosylase") (OGG1) is the primary enzyme responsible for the excision of the oxidized guanine during DNA repair. OGG1 finds and binds to an 8-OHdG within a few seconds.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid33171795-101) However, OGG1 does not immediately excise 8-OHdG. In HeLa cells half maximum removal of 8-OHdG occurs in 30 minutes,[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid15365186-102) and in irradiated mice, the 8-OHdGs induced in the mouse liver are removed with a half-life of 11 minutes.[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hamilton-97)
When OGG1 is present at an oxidized guanine within a methylated CpG site it recruits [TET1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TET_enzymes "TET enzymes") to the 8-OHdG lesion (see Figure). This allows TET1 to demethylate an adjacent methylated cytosine. Demethylation of cytosine is an epigenetic alteration.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
As an example, when human mammary epithelial cells were treated with H2O2 for six hours, 8-OHdG increased about 3.5-fold in DNA and this caused about 80% demethylation of the 5-methylcytosines in the genome.[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Zhou-99) Demethylation of CpGs in a gene promoter by [TET enzyme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TET_enzymes "TET enzymes") activity increases transcription of the gene into messenger RNA.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid24108092-103) In cells treated with H2O2, one particular gene was examined, [_BACE1_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beta-secretase_1 "Beta-secretase 1").[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Zhou-99) The methylation level of the _BACE1_ CpG island was reduced (an epigenetic alteration) and this allowed about 6.5 fold increase of expression of _BACE1_ messenger RNA.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
While six-hour incubation with H2O2 causes considerable demethylation of 5-mCpG sites, shorter times of H2O2 incubation appear to promote other epigenetic alterations. Treatment of cells with H2O2 for 30 minutes causes the mismatch repair protein heterodimer MSH2-MSH6 to recruit DNA methyltransferase 1 ([DNMT1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNMT1 "DNMT1")) to sites of some kinds of oxidative DNA damage.[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid26186941-104) This could cause increased methylation of cytosines (epigenetic alterations) at these locations. 
Jiang et al.[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Jiang-105) treated [HEK 293 cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HEK_293_cells "HEK 293 cells") with agents causing oxidative DNA damage, ([potassium bromate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_bromate "Potassium bromate") (KBrO3) or [potassium chromate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potassium_chromate "Potassium chromate") (K2CrO4)). [Base excision repair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_excision_repair "Base excision repair") (BER) of oxidative damage occurred with the DNA repair enzyme [polymerase beta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_polymerase "DNA polymerase") localizing to oxidized guanines. Polymerase beta is the main human polymerase in short-patch BER of oxidative DNA damage. Jiang et al.[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Jiang-105) also found that polymerase beta recruited the [DNA methyltransferase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methyltransferase "DNA methyltransferase") protein DNMT3b to BER repair sites. They then evaluated the methylation pattern at the single nucleotide level in a small region of DNA including the [promoter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promoter_\(genetics\) "Promoter \(genetics\)") region and the early transcription region of the [BRCA1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BRCA1 "BRCA1") gene. Oxidative DNA damage from bromate modulated the DNA methylation pattern (caused epigenetic alterations) at CpG sites within the region of DNA studied. In untreated cells, CpGs located at −189, −134, −29, −19, +16, and +19 of the BRCA1 gene had methylated cytosines (where numbering is from the [messenger RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA "Messenger RNA") transcription start site, and negative numbers indicate nucleotides in the upstream [promoter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promoter_\(genetics\) "Promoter \(genetics\)") region). Bromate treatment-induced oxidation resulted in the loss of cytosine methylation at −189, −134, +16 and +19 while also leading to the formation of new methylation at the CpGs located at −80, −55, −21 and +8 after DNA repair was allowed. 
#### Homologous recombinational repair alters epigenetic markers
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Homologous recombinational repair alters epigenetic markers")]
At least four articles report the recruitment of [DNA methyltransferase 1 (DNMT1)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methyltransferase "DNA methyltransferase") to sites of DNA double-strand breaks.[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid15956212-106)[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Cuozzo-85)[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid18704159-107)[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid20940144-108) During [homologous recombinational repair (HR)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homologous_recombination "Homologous recombination") of the double-strand break, the involvement of DNMT1 causes the two repaired strands of DNA to have different levels of methylated cytosines. One strand becomes frequently methylated at about 21 CpG sites downstream of the repaired double-strand break. The other DNA strand loses methylation at about six CpG sites that were previously methylated downstream of the double-strand break, as well as losing methylation at about five CpG sites that were previously methylated upstream of the double-strand break. When the chromosome is replicated, this gives rise to one daughter chromosome that is heavily methylated downstream of the previous break site and one that is unmethylated in the region both upstream and downstream of the previous break site. With respect to the gene that was broken by the double-strand break, half of the progeny cells express that gene at a high level and in the other half of the progeny cells expression of that gene is repressed. When clones of these cells were maintained for three years, the new methylation patterns were maintained over that time period.[[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid27629060-109)
In mice with a CRISPR-mediated homology-directed recombination insertion in their genome there were a large number of increased methylations of CpG sites within the double-strand break-associated insertion.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid33267773-110)
#### Non-homologous end joining can cause some epigenetic marker alterations
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Non-homologous end joining can cause some epigenetic marker alterations")]
[Non-homologous end joining](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-homologous_end_joining "Non-homologous end joining") (NHEJ) repair of a double-strand break can cause a small number of demethylations of pre-existing cytosine DNA methylations downstream of the repaired double-strand break.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Cuozzo-85) Further work by Allen et al.[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid28423717-111) showed that NHEJ of a DNA double-strand break in a cell could give rise to some progeny cells having repressed expression of the gene harboring the initial double-strand break and some progeny having high expression of that gene due to epigenetic alterations associated with NHEJ repair. The frequency of epigenetic alterations causing repression of a gene after an NHEJ repair of a DNA double-strand break in that gene may be about 0.9%.[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid18704159-107)
### Techniques used to study epigenetics
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Techniques used to study epigenetics")]
Epigenetic research uses a wide range of [molecular biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_biology "Molecular biology") techniques to further understanding of epigenetic phenomena. These techniques include [chromatin immunoprecipitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromatin_immunoprecipitation "Chromatin immunoprecipitation") (together with its large-scale variants [ChIP-on-chip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ChIP-on-chip "ChIP-on-chip") and [ChIP-Seq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ChIP-Seq "ChIP-Seq")), [fluorescent in situ hybridization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluorescent_in_situ_hybridization "Fluorescent in situ hybridization"), methylation-sensitive [restriction enzymes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restriction_enzymes "Restriction enzymes"), DNA adenine methyltransferase identification ([DamID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DamID "DamID")) and [bisulfite sequencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bisulfite_sequencing "Bisulfite sequencing").[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-verma-112) Furthermore, the use of [bioinformatics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioinformatics "Bioinformatics") methods has a role in [computational epigenetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_epigenetics "Computational epigenetics").[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-verma-112)
#### Chromatin immunoprecipitation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Chromatin immunoprecipitation")]
Chromatin immunoprecipitation (ChIP) has helped bridge the gap between DNA and epigenetic interactions.[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Abcam-113) With the use of ChIP, researchers are able to make findings in regards to gene regulation, transcription mechanisms, and chromatin structure.[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Abcam-113)
#### Fluorescent _in situ_ hybridization
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section: Fluorescent in situ hybridization")]
Fluorescent _in situ_ hybridization (FISH) is very important to understand epigenetic mechanisms.[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Chaumeil_2008-114) FISH can be used to find the location of genes on chromosomes, as well as finding noncoding RNAs.[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Chaumeil_2008-114)[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-O'Connor_2008-115) FISH is predominantly used for detecting chromosomal abnormalities in humans.[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-O'Connor_2008-115)
#### Methylation-sensitive restriction enzymes
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Methylation-sensitive restriction enzymes")]
Methylation sensitive restriction enzymes paired with PCR is a way to evaluate methylation in DNA - specifically the CpG sites.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hashimoto_2007-116) If DNA is methylated, the restriction enzymes will not cleave the strand.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hashimoto_2007-116) Contrarily, if the DNA is not methylated, the enzymes will cleave the strand and it will be amplified by PCR.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hashimoto_2007-116)
#### Bisulfite sequencing
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section: Bisulfite sequencing")]
Bisulfite sequencing is another way to evaluate DNA methylation. Cytosine will be changed to uracil from being treated with sodium bisulfite, whereas methylated cytosines will not be affected.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hashimoto_2007-116)[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Li-Byarlay_et_al_2020-117)[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-ReferenceC-118)
#### Nanopore sequencing
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Nanopore sequencing")]
Certain sequencing methods, such as [nanopore sequencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanopore_sequencing "Nanopore sequencing"), allow sequencing of native DNA. Native (=unamplified) DNA retains the epigenetic modifications which would otherwise be lost during the amplification step. Nanopore basecaller models can distinguish between the signals obtained for epigenetically modified bases and unaltered based and provide an epigenetic profile in addition to the sequencing result.[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-119)
### Structural inheritance
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Structural inheritance")]
Further information: [Structural inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Structural_inheritance "Structural inheritance")
In [ciliates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciliate "Ciliate") such as _[Tetrahymena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetrahymena "Tetrahymena")_ and _[Paramecium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramecium "Paramecium")_ , genetically identical cells show heritable differences in the patterns of ciliary rows on their cell surface. Experimentally altered patterns can be transmitted to daughter cells. It seems existing structures act as templates for new structures. The mechanisms of such inheritance are unclear, but reasons exist to assume that multicellular organisms also use existing cell structures to assemble new ones.[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid1804215-120)[[121]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-isbn0-19-515619-6-121)[[122]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-isbn0-262-65063-0-122)
### Nucleosome positioning
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Nucleosome positioning")]
Eukaryotic genomes have numerous [nucleosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosomes "Nucleosomes"). Nucleosome position is not random, and determine the accessibility of DNA to regulatory proteins. Promoters active in different tissues have been shown to have different nucleosome positioning features.[[123]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-123) This determines differences in gene expression and cell differentiation. It has been shown that at least some nucleosomes are retained in sperm cells (where most but not all histones are replaced by [protamines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protamines "Protamines")). Thus nucleosome positioning is to some degree inheritable. Recent studies have uncovered connections between nucleosome positioning and other epigenetic factors, such as DNA methylation and hydroxymethylation.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Teif_2014-124)
### Histone variants
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: Histone variants")]
Different [histone variants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_variants "Histone variants") are incorporated into specific regions of the genome non-randomly. Their differential biochemical characteristics can affect genome functions via their roles in gene regulation,[[125]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-125) and maintenance of chromosome structures.[[126]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-126)
### Genomic architecture
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Genomic architecture")]
The three-dimensional configuration of the genome (the 3D genome) is complex, dynamic and crucial for regulating genomic function and nuclear processes such as DNA replication, transcription and DNA-damage repair.[[127]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-127)
## Functions and consequences
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Functions and consequences")]
### In the brain
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: In the brain")]
See also: [§ Addiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#Addiction), and [§ Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#Depression)
#### Memory
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Memory")]
Main article: [Epigenetics in learning and memory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics_in_learning_and_memory "Epigenetics in learning and memory")
[Memory formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encoding_\(memory\) "Encoding \(memory\)") and maintenance are due to epigenetic alterations that cause the required dynamic changes in [gene transcription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_transcription "Gene transcription") that create and renew memory in neurons.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Bernstein-30)
An event can set off a chain of reactions that result in altered methylations of a large set of genes in neurons, which give a representation of the event, a memory.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Bernstein-30)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Brain_regions_in_memory_formation_updated.jpg/250px-Brain_regions_in_memory_formation_updated.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Brain_regions_in_memory_formation_updated.jpg)Including medial prefrontal cortex (mPFC)
Areas of the brain important in the formation of memories include the hippocampus, medial prefrontal cortex (mPFC), anterior cingulate cortex and amygdala, as shown in the diagram of the human brain in this section.[[128]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid28386011-128)
When a strong memory is created, as in a rat subjected to [contextual fear conditioning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fear_conditioning "Fear conditioning") (CFC), one of the earliest events to occur is that more than 100 DNA double-strand breaks are formed by [topoisomerase IIB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topoisomerase "Topoisomerase") in neurons of the hippocampus and the medial prefrontal cortex (mPFC).[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Stott-129) These double-strand breaks are at specific locations that allow activation of transcription of [immediate early genes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immediate_early_genes "Immediate early genes") (IEGs) that are important in memory formation, allowing their expression in [mRNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA "Messenger RNA"), with peak mRNA transcription at seven to ten minutes after CFC.[[129]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Stott-129)[[130]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid35776545-130)
Two important IEGs in memory formation are _[EGR1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EGR1 "EGR1")_[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid10357227-131) and [the alternative promoter variant of _DNMT3A_ , _DNMT3A2_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methyltransferase "DNA methyltransferase").[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid22751036-132) EGR1 protein binds to DNA at its binding motifs, 5′-GCGTGGGCG-3′ or 5′-GCGGGGGCGG-3', and there are about 12,000 genome locations at which EGR1 protein can bind.[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Sun-133) EGR1 protein binds to DNA in gene [promoter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promoter_\(genetics\) "Promoter \(genetics\)") and [enhancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enhancer_\(genetics\) "Enhancer \(genetics\)") regions. EGR1 recruits the demethylating enzyme [TET1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TET_enzymes "TET enzymes") to an association, and brings TET1 to about 600 locations on the genome where TET1 can then demethylate and activate the associated genes.[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Sun-133)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b9/Cytosine_and_5-methylcytosine.svg/250px-Cytosine_and_5-methylcytosine.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cytosine_and_5-methylcytosine.svg)Cytosine and 5-methylcytosine
The DNA methyltransferases DNMT3A1, DNMT3A2 and DNMT3B can all methylate cytosines (see image this section) at CpG sites in or near the promoters of genes. As shown by Manzo et al.,[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid29074627-134) these three DNA methyltransferases differ in their genomic binding locations and DNA methylation activity at different regulatory sites. Manzo et al. located 3,970 genome regions exclusively enriched for DNMT3A1, 3,838 regions for DNMT3A2 and 3,432 regions for DNMT3B. When DNMT3A2 is newly induced as an IEG (when neurons are activated), many new cytosine methylations occur, presumably in the target regions of DNMT3A2. Oliviera et al.[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid22751036-132) found that the neuronal activity-inducible IEG levels of Dnmt3a2 in the hippocampus determined the ability to form long-term memories. 
Rats form long-term associative memories after [contextual fear conditioning (CFC)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fear_conditioning "Fear conditioning").[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid25324744-135) Duke et al.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid28620075-29) found that 24 hours after CFC in rats, in hippocampus neurons, 2,097 genes (9.17% of the genes in the rat genome) had altered methylation. When newly methylated cytosines are present in CpG sites in the promoter regions of genes, the genes are often repressed, and when newly demethylated cytosines are present the genes may be activated.[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid22781841-136) After CFC, there were 1,048 genes with reduced mRNA expression and 564 genes with upregulated mRNA expression. Similarly, when mice undergo CFC, one hour later in the hippocampus region of the mouse brain there are 675 demethylated genes and 613 hypermethylated genes.[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Halder-137) However, memories do not remain in the hippocampus, but after four or five weeks the memories are stored in the anterior cingulate cortex.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid15131309-138) In the studies on mice after CFC, Halder et al.[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Halder-137) showed that four weeks after CFC there were at least 1,000 differentially methylated genes and more than 1,000 differentially expressed genes in the anterior cingulate cortex, while at the same time the altered methylations in the hippocampus were reversed. 
The epigenetic alteration of methylation after a new memory is established creates a different pool of nuclear mRNAs. As reviewed by Bernstein,[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Bernstein-30) the epigenetically determined new mix of nuclear [mRNAs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNA "Messenger RNA") are often packaged into neuronal granules, or [messenger RNP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messenger_RNP "Messenger RNP"), consisting of mRNA, [small and large ribosomal subunits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ribosome "Ribosome"), translation initiation factors and RNA-binding proteins that regulate mRNA function.
