These neuronal granules are transported from the neuron nucleus and are directed, according to 3′ untranslated regions of the mRNA in the granules (their "zip codes"), to neuronal [dendrites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dendrite "Dendrite"). Roughly 2,500 mRNAs may be localized to the dendrites of hippocampal pyramidal neurons and perhaps 450 transcripts are in excitatory presynaptic nerve terminals (dendritic spines). The altered assortments of transcripts (dependent on epigenetic alterations in the neuron nucleus) have different sensitivities in response to signals, which is the basis of altered synaptic plasticity. Altered synaptic plasticity is often considered the neurochemical foundation of learning and memory. 
#### Aging
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Aging")]
See also: [DNA methylation § In aging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation#In_aging "DNA methylation"), and [Hallmarks of aging § Epigenomic alterations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hallmarks_of_aging#Epigenomic_alterations "Hallmarks of aging")
Epigenetics play a major role in [brain aging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brain_aging "Brain aging") and age-related cognitive decline, with relevance to [life extension](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_extension "Life extension").[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-139)[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-140)[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-141)[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-142)[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-143)
#### Other and general
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: Other and general")]
In adulthood, changes in the [epigenome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenome "Epigenome") are important for various higher cognitive functions. Dysregulation of epigenetic mechanisms is implicated in [neurodegenerative disorders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurodegenerative_disorders "Neurodegenerative disorders") and diseases. Epigenetic modifications in [neurons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuron "Neuron") are dynamic and reversible.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-144) Epigenetic regulation impacts neuronal action, affecting learning, memory, and other [cognitive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive "Cognitive") processes.[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-145)
Early events, including during [embryonic development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryonic_development "Embryonic development"), can influence development, cognition, and health outcomes through [epigenetic mechanisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetic_mechanisms&action=edit&redlink=1 "Epigenetic mechanisms \(page does not exist\)").[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-146)
Epigenetic mechanisms have been proposed as "a potential molecular mechanism for effects of endogenous [hormones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hormone "Hormone") on the organization of developing brain circuits".[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-147)
[Nutrients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrients "Nutrients") could interact with the epigenome to "protect or boost cognitive processes across the lifespan".[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-148)[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-149)
A review suggests [neurobiological effects of physical exercise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurobiological_effects_of_physical_exercise "Neurobiological effects of physical exercise") via [epigenetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics_of_physical_exercise "Epigenetics of physical exercise") seem "central to building an 'epigenetic memory' to influence long-term brain function and behavior" and may even be heritable.[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-150)
With the axo-ciliary [synapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synapse "Synapse"), there is communication between [serotonergic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serotonin "Serotonin") [axons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axon "Axon") and antenna-like [primary cilia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_cilia "Primary cilia") of [CA1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hippocampus_anatomy#Basic_hippocampal_circuit "Hippocampus anatomy") [pyramidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramidal_cell "Pyramidal cell") [neurons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuron "Neuron") that alters the neuron's [epigenetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetic "Epigenetic") state in the [nucleus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_nucleus "Cell nucleus") via the signalling distinct from that at the [plasma membrane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_membrane "Plasma membrane") (and longer-term).[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-151)[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-152)
Epigenetics also play a major role in the [brain evolution in and to humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_the_brain#Genetic_factors_of_recent_evolution "Evolution of the brain").[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-153)
### Development
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: Development")]
See also: [Epigenetics of human development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics_of_human_development "Epigenetics of human development")
Developmental epigenetics can be divided into predetermined and probabilistic epigenesis. Predetermined epigenesis is a unidirectional movement from structural development in DNA to the functional maturation of the protein. "Predetermined" here means that development is scripted and predictable. Probabilistic epigenesis on the other hand is a bidirectional structure-function development with experiences and external molding development.[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Griesemer_2005-154)
Somatic epigenetic inheritance, particularly through DNA and histone covalent modifications and [nucleosome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleosome "Nucleosome") repositioning, is very important in the development of multicellular eukaryotic organisms.[[124]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Teif_2014-124) The genome sequence is static (with some notable exceptions), but cells differentiate into many different types, which perform different functions, and respond differently to the environment and intercellular signaling. Thus, as individuals develop, [morphogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphogen "Morphogen") activate or silence genes in an epigenetically heritable fashion, giving cells a memory. In mammals, most cells terminally differentiate, with only [stem cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_cells "Stem cells") retaining the ability to differentiate into several cell types ("totipotency" and "multipotency"). In [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal "Mammal"), some stem cells continue producing newly differentiated cells throughout life, such as in [neurogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetic_Regulation_of_Neurogenesis "Epigenetic Regulation of Neurogenesis"), but mammals are not able to respond to loss of some tissues, for example, the inability to regenerate limbs, which some other animals are capable of. Epigenetic modifications regulate the transition from neural stem cells to glial progenitor cells (for example, differentiation into oligodendrocytes is regulated by the deacetylation and methylation of histones).[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-155) Unlike animals, plant cells do not terminally differentiate, remaining totipotent with the ability to give rise to a new individual plant. While plants do utilize many of the same epigenetic mechanisms as animals, such as [chromatin remodeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromatin_remodeling "Chromatin remodeling"), it has been hypothesized that some kinds of plant cells do not use or require "cellular memories", resetting their gene expression patterns using positional information from the environment and surrounding cells to determine their fate.[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17194589-156)
Epigenetic changes can occur in response to environmental exposure – for example, maternal dietary supplementation with [genistein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genistein "Genistein") (250 mg/kg) have epigenetic changes affecting expression of the [agouti gene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agouti_gene "Agouti gene"), which affects their fur color, weight, and propensity to develop cancer.[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid12163699-157)[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-waterland-158)[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-159) Ongoing research is focused on exploring the impact of other known [teratogens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teratogen "Teratogen"), such as [diabetic embryopathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetic_embryopathy "Diabetic embryopathy"), on [methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methylation "Methylation") signatures.[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-160)
Controversial results from one study suggested that traumatic experiences might produce an epigenetic signal that is capable of being passed to future generations. Mice were trained, using foot shocks, to fear a cherry blossom odor. The investigators reported that the mouse offspring had an increased aversion to this specific odor.[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-161)[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-162) They suggested epigenetic changes that increase gene expression, rather than in DNA itself, in a gene, M71, that governs the functioning of an odor receptor in the nose that responds specifically to this cherry blossom smell. There were physical changes that correlated with olfactory (smell) function in the brains of the trained mice and their descendants. Several criticisms were reported, including the study's low statistical power as evidence of some irregularity such as bias in reporting results.[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Francis_2014-163) Due to limits of sample size, there is a probability that an effect will not be demonstrated to within statistical significance even if it exists. The criticism suggested that the probability that all the experiments reported would show positive results if an identical protocol was followed, assuming the claimed effects exist, is merely 0.4%. The authors also did not indicate which mice were siblings, and treated all of the mice as statistically independent.[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-164) The original researchers pointed out negative results in the paper's appendix that the criticism omitted in its calculations, and undertook to track which mice were siblings in the future.[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-165)
### Transgenerational
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: Transgenerational")]
Main article: [Transgenerational epigenetic inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgenerational_epigenetic_inheritance "Transgenerational epigenetic inheritance")
Epigenetic mechanisms were a necessary part of the evolutionary origin of [cell differentiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cell_differentiation "Cell differentiation").[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-isbn0-19-854968-7-166)[_[need quotation to verify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_] Although epigenetics in multicellular organisms is generally thought to be a mechanism involved in differentiation, with epigenetic patterns "reset" when organisms reproduce, there have been some observations of transgenerational epigenetic inheritance (e.g., the phenomenon of [paramutation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramutation "Paramutation") observed in [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize "Maize")). Although most of these multigenerational epigenetic traits are gradually lost over several generations, the possibility remains that multigenerational epigenetics could be another aspect to [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution "Evolution") and adaptation. As mentioned above, some define epigenetics as heritable. 
A sequestered germ line or [Weismann barrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weismann_barrier "Weismann barrier") is specific to animals, and epigenetic inheritance is more common in plants and microbes. [Eva Jablonka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eva_Jablonka "Eva Jablonka"), [Marion J. Lamb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_J._Lamb "Marion J. Lamb") and Étienne Danchin have argued that these effects may require enhancements to the standard conceptual framework of the [modern synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_synthesis_\(20th_century\) "Modern synthesis \(20th century\)") and have called for an [extended evolutionary synthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extended_evolutionary_synthesis "Extended evolutionary synthesis").[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-isbn0-262-10107-6-167)[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-168)[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-169) Other evolutionary biologists, such as [John Maynard Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Maynard_Smith "John Maynard Smith"), have incorporated epigenetic inheritance into [population-genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_genetics "Population genetics") models[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-170) or are openly skeptical of the extended evolutionary synthesis ([Michael Lynch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Lynch_\(geneticist\) "Michael Lynch \(geneticist\)")).[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-171) Thomas Dickins and Qazi Rahman state that epigenetic mechanisms such as DNA methylation and histone modification are genetically inherited under the control of [natural selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_selection "Natural selection") and therefore fit under the earlier ["modern synthesis"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_synthesis_\(20th_century\) "Modern synthesis \(20th century\)").[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-172)
Two important ways in which epigenetic inheritance can differ from traditional genetic inheritance, with important consequences for evolution, are: 
  * rates of epimutation can be much faster than rates of mutation[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-rando_and_verstrepen-173)
  * the epimutations are more easily reversible[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-174)


In plants, heritable DNA methylation mutations are 100,000 times more likely to occur compared to DNA mutations.[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-van_der_Graaf_et_al-175) An epigenetically inherited element such as the [PSI+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSI_\(prion\) "PSI \(prion\)") system can act as a "stop-gap", good enough for short-term adaptation that allows the lineage to survive for long enough for mutation and/or recombination to [genetically assimilate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_assimilation "Genetic assimilation") the adaptive phenotypic change.[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-176) The existence of this possibility increases the [evolvability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolvability "Evolvability") of a species. 
More than 100 cases of [transgenerational epigenetic inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgenerational_epigenetic_inheritance "Transgenerational epigenetic inheritance") phenomena have been reported in a wide range of organisms, including prokaryotes, plants, and animals.[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Jablonka09-177) For instance, [mourning-cloak butterflies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymphalis_antiopa "Nymphalis antiopa") will change color through hormone changes in response to experimentation of varying temperatures.[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-178)
The filamentous fungus _Neurospora crassa_ is a prominent model system for understanding the control and function of cytosine methylation. In this organism, DNA methylation is associated with relics of a genome-defense system called RIP (repeat-induced point mutation) and silences gene expression by inhibiting transcription elongation.[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19092133-179)
The [yeast prion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeast_prion "Yeast prion") PSI is generated by a conformational change of a translation termination factor, which is then inherited by daughter cells. This can provide a survival advantage under adverse conditions, exemplifying epigenetic regulation which enables unicellular organisms to respond rapidly to environmental stress. Prions can be viewed as epigenetic agents capable of inducing a phenotypic change without modification of the genome.[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-JorgTost-180)
Direct detection of epigenetic marks in microorganisms is possible with [single molecule real time sequencing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_molecule_real_time_sequencing "Single molecule real time sequencing"), in which polymerase sensitivity allows for measuring methylation and other modifications as a DNA molecule is being sequenced.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-181) Several projects have demonstrated the ability to collect genome-wide epigenetic data in bacteria.[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-182)[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-183)[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-184)[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-185)
## Epigenetics in bacteria
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Epigenetics in bacteria")]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/eb/Escherichia_coli_flagella_TEM.png/250px-Escherichia_coli_flagella_TEM.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Escherichia_coli_flagella_TEM.png)_Escherichia coli_ bacteria
While epigenetics is of fundamental importance in [eukaryotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eukaryote "Eukaryote"), especially [metazoans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multicellular_organism "Multicellular organism"), it plays a different role in bacteria.[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-186) Most importantly, eukaryotes use epigenetic mechanisms primarily to regulate gene expression which bacteria rarely do. However, bacteria make widespread use of postreplicative DNA methylation for the epigenetic control of DNA-protein interactions. Bacteria also use DNA [adenine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adenine "Adenine") methylation (rather than DNA [cytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytosine "Cytosine") methylation) as an epigenetic signal. DNA adenine methylation is important in bacteria virulence in organisms such as _[Escherichia coli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escherichia_coli "Escherichia coli")_ , _[Salmonella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salmonella "Salmonella"), [Vibrio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vibrio "Vibrio"), [Yersinia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yersinia "Yersinia"), [Haemophilus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haemophilus "Haemophilus")_, and _[Brucella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brucella "Brucella")_. In _[Alphaproteobacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphaproteobacteria "Alphaproteobacteria")_ , methylation of adenine regulates the cell cycle and couples gene transcription to DNA replication. In _[Gammaproteobacteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gammaproteobacteria "Gammaproteobacteria")_ , adenine methylation provides signals for DNA replication, chromosome segregation, mismatch repair, packaging of bacteriophage, transposase activity and regulation of gene expression.[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-JorgTost-180)[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Casadesus-187) There exists a genetic switch controlling _[Streptococcus pneumoniae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streptococcus_pneumoniae "Streptococcus pneumoniae")_ (the pneumococcus) that allows the bacterium to randomly change its characteristics into six alternative states that could pave the way to improved vaccines. Each form is randomly generated by a phase variable methylation system. The ability of the pneumococcus to cause deadly infections is different in each of these six states. Similar systems exist in other bacterial genera.[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-MansoOggioni2014-188) In [Bacillota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacillota "Bacillota") such as _[Clostridioides difficile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clostridioides_difficile_\(bacteria\) "Clostridioides difficile \(bacteria\)"),_ adenine methylation regulates [sporulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spore "Spore"), [biofilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofilm "Biofilm") formation and host-adaptation.[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-189)
## Medicine
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Medicine")]
Epigenetics has many and varied potential medical applications.[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid21447282-190)
### Twins
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Twins")]
Direct comparisons of identical twins constitute an optimal model for interrogating [environmental epigenetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_epigenetics "Environmental epigenetics"). In the case of humans with different environmental exposures, monozygotic (identical) twins were epigenetically indistinguishable during their early years, while older twins had remarkable differences in the overall content and genomic distribution of 5-methylcytosine DNA and histone acetylation.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Moore_2015-10) The twin pairs who had spent less of their lifetime together and/or had greater differences in their medical histories were those who showed the largest differences in their levels of [5-methylcytosine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5-methylcytosine "5-methylcytosine") DNA and [acetylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acetylation "Acetylation") of [histones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histones "Histones") H3 and H4.[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid16009939-191)
Dizygotic (fraternal) and monozygotic (identical) twins show evidence of epigenetic influence in humans.[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid16009939-191)[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19151718-192)[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-193) DNA sequence differences that would be abundant in a singleton-based study do not interfere with the analysis. Environmental differences can produce long-term epigenetic effects, and different developmental monozygotic twin subtypes may be different with respect to their susceptibility to be discordant from an epigenetic point of view.[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19653134-194)
A [high-throughput](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-throughput_screening "High-throughput screening") study, which denotes technology that looks at extensive genetic markers, focused on epigenetic differences between monozygotic twins to compare global and locus-specific changes in [DNA methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation "DNA methylation") and histone modifications in a sample of 40 monozygotic twin pairs.[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid16009939-191) In this case, only healthy twin pairs were studied, but a wide range of ages was represented, between 3 and 74 years. One of the major conclusions from this study was that there is an age-dependent accumulation of epigenetic differences between the two siblings of twin pairs. This accumulation suggests the existence of epigenetic "drift". _Epigenetic drift_ is the term given to epigenetic modifications as they occur as a direct function with age. While age is a known risk factor for many diseases, age-related methylation has been found to occur differentially at specific sites along the genome. Over time, this can result in measurable differences between biological and chronological age. Epigenetic changes have been found to be reflective of [lifestyle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifestyle_\(social_sciences\) "Lifestyle \(social sciences\)") and may act as functional [biomarkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomarker "Biomarker") of disease before clinical [threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reference_range "Reference range") is reached.[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-195)
A more recent study, where 114 monozygotic twins and 80 dizygotic twins were analyzed for the DNA methylation status of around 6000 unique genomic regions, concluded that epigenetic similarity at the time of blastocyst splitting may also contribute to phenotypic similarities in monozygotic co-twins. This supports the notion that [microenvironment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microenvironment_\(biology\) "Microenvironment \(biology\)") at early stages of embryonic development can be quite important for the establishment of epigenetic marks.[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid19151718-192) Congenital genetic disease is well understood and it is clear that epigenetics can play a role, for example, in the case of [Angelman syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelman_syndrome "Angelman syndrome") and [Prader–Willi syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prader%E2%80%93Willi_syndrome "Prader–Willi syndrome"). These are normal genetic diseases caused by gene deletions or inactivation of the genes but are unusually common because individuals are essentially [hemizygous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemizygous "Hemizygous") because of [genomic imprinting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genomic_imprinting "Genomic imprinting"), and therefore a single gene knock out is sufficient to cause the disease, where most cases would require both copies to be knocked out.[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-196)
### Genomic imprinting
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Genomic imprinting")]
Further information: [Genomic imprinting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genomic_imprinting "Genomic imprinting")
Some human disorders are associated with genomic imprinting, a phenomenon in mammals where the father and mother contribute different epigenetic patterns for specific genomic loci in their [germ cells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germ_cells "Germ cells").[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid17121465-197) The best-known case of imprinting in human disorders is that of [Angelman syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angelman_syndrome "Angelman syndrome") and [Prader–Willi syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prader%E2%80%93Willi_syndrome "Prader–Willi syndrome") – both can be produced by the same genetic mutation, [chromosome 15q partial deletion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromosome_15q_partial_deletion "Chromosome 15q partial deletion"), and the particular syndrome that will develop depends on whether the mutation is inherited from the child's mother or from their father.[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid2564739-198)
In the [Överkalix study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%96verkalix_study "Överkalix study"), paternal (but not maternal) grandsons[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-paternal-grandson-199) of Swedish men who were exposed during preadolescence to famine in the 19th century were less likely to die of cardiovascular disease. If food was plentiful, then [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes "Diabetes") mortality in the grandchildren increased, suggesting that this was a transgenerational epigenetic inheritance.[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid16391557-200) The opposite effect was observed for females – the paternal (but not maternal) granddaughters of women who experienced famine while in the womb (and therefore while their eggs were being formed) lived shorter lives on average.[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-201)
### Examples of drugs altering gene expression from epigenetic events
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Examples of drugs altering gene expression from epigenetic events")]
See also: [Epigenetic Priming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetic_Priming "Epigenetic Priming")
The use of beta-lactam [antibiotics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibiotics "Antibiotics") can alter glutamate receptor activity and the action of cyclosporine on multiple transcription factors. Additionally, [lithium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium_\(medication\) "Lithium \(medication\)") can impact autophagy of aberrant proteins, and [opioid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opioid "Opioid") drugs via chronic use can increase the expression of genes associated with addictive phenotypes.[[202]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-202)
Parental [nutrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrition "Nutrition"), in utero exposure to stress or [endocrine disrupting chemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endocrine_disruptor "Endocrine disruptor"),[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-203) male-induced maternal effects such as the attraction of differential mate quality, and maternal as well as paternal age, and offspring gender could all possibly influence whether a germline epimutation is ultimately expressed in offspring and the degree to which intergenerational inheritance remains stable throughout posterity.[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-ReferenceB-204) However, whether and to what extent epigenetic effects can be transmitted across generations remains unclear, particularly in humans.[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-PlominDeFries2012-205)[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-206)
### Addiction
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Addiction")]
[Addiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Addiction "Addiction") is a disorder of the brain's [reward system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reward_system "Reward system") which arises through [transcriptional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcriptional "Transcriptional") and neuroepigenetic mechanisms and occurs over time from chronically high levels of exposure to an addictive stimulus (e.g., morphine, cocaine, sexual intercourse, gambling).[[207]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Nestler-207)[[208]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Cheron2021-208)[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-G9a_reverses_%CE%94FosB_plasticity-209) Transgenerational epigenetic inheritance of addictive [phenotypes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotypes "Phenotypes") has been noted to occur in preclinical studies.[[210]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid23920159-210)[[211]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid26572641-211) However, robust evidence in support of the persistence of epigenetic effects across multiple generations has yet to be established in humans; for example, an epigenetic effect of prenatal exposure to smoking that is observed in great-grandchildren who had not been exposed.[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-PlominDeFries2012-205)
## Research
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=42 "Edit section: Research")]
The two forms of heritable information, namely genetic and epigenetic, are collectively called dual inheritance. Members of the APOBEC/AID family of [cytosine deaminases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cytosine_deaminase "Cytosine deaminase") may concurrently influence genetic and epigenetic inheritance using similar molecular mechanisms, and may be a point of crosstalk between these conceptually compartmentalized processes.[[212]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid20800313-212)
[Fluoroquinolone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluoroquinolone "Fluoroquinolone") antibiotics induce epigenetic changes in [mammalian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammalian "Mammalian") cells through iron [chelation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chelation "Chelation"). This leads to epigenetic effects through inhibition of α-ketoglutarate-dependent [dioxygenases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dioxygenases "Dioxygenases") that require [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron "Iron") as a co-factor.[[213]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-213)
Various pharmacological agents are applied for the production of induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSC) or maintain the embryonic stem cell (ESC) phenotypic via epigenetic approach. Adult stem cells like bone marrow stem cells have also shown a potential to differentiate into cardiac competent cells when treated with G9a histone methyltransferase inhibitor BIX01294.[[214]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-214)[[215]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-215)
Cell plasticity, which is the adaptation of cells to stimuli without changes in their genetic code, requires epigenetic changes. These have been observed in cell plasticity in cancer cells during epithelial-to-mesenchymal transition[[216]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-216) and also in immune cells, such as macrophages.[[217]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-217) Interestingly, metabolic changes underlie these adaptations, since various metabolites play crucial roles in the chemistry of epigenetic marks. This includes for instance alpha-ketoglutarate, which is required for histone demethylation, and acetyl-Coenzyme A, which is required for histone acetylation. 
### Epigenome editing
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Epigenome editing")]
Main article: [Epigenome editing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenome_editing "Epigenome editing")
Epigenetic regulation of gene expression that could be altered or used in [epigenome editing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenome_editing "Epigenome editing") are or include [mRNA/lncRNA modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=MRNA_modification&action=edit&redlink=1 "MRNA modification \(page does not exist\)"), [DNA methylation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA_methylation "DNA methylation") modification and [histone modification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Histone_modification "Histone modification").[[218]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-218)[[219]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-219)[[220]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-220)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=44 "Edit section: CpG sites, SNPs and biological traits")]
Methylation is a widely characterized mechanism of genetic regulation that can determine biological traits. However, strong experimental evidences correlate methylation patterns in SNPs as an important additional feature for the classical activation/inhibition epigenetic dogma. Molecular interaction data, supported by colocalization analyses, identify multiple nuclear regulatory pathways, linking sequence variation to disturbances in DNA methylation and molecular and phenotypic variation.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221)
####  _UBASH3B_ locus
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=45 "Edit section: UBASH3B locus")]
_UBASH3B_ encodes a protein with tyrosine phosphatase activity, which has been previously linked to advanced neoplasia.[[222]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-222) SNP rs7115089 was identified as influencing DNA methylation and expression of this locus, as well as and Body Mass Index (BMI).[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221) In fact, SNP rs7115089 is strongly associated with BMI[[223]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-223) and with genetic variants linked to other cardiovascular and metabolic traits in GWASs.[[224]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-224)[[225]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-225)[[226]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-226) New studies suggesting _UBASH3B_ as a potential mediator of adiposity and cardiometabolic disease.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221) In addition, animal models demonstrated that _UBASH3B_ expression is an indicator of caloric restriction that may drive programmed susceptibility to obesity and it is associated with other measures of adiposity in human peripherical blood.[[227]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-227)
####  _NFKBIE_ locus
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=46 "Edit section: NFKBIE locus")]
SNP rs730775 is located in the first intron of _NFKBIE_ and is a _cis_ eQTL for _NFKBIE_ in whole blood.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221) Nuclear factor (NF)-κB inhibitor ε (NFKBIE) directly inhibits NF-κB1 activity and is significantly co-expressed with NF-κB1, also, it is associated with rheumatoid arthritis.[[228]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-228) Colocalization analysis supports that variants for the majority of the CpG sites in SNP rs730775 cause genetic variation at the _NFKBIE_ locus which is suggestible linked to rheumatoid arthritis through _trans_ acting regulation of DNA methylation by NF-κB.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221)
####  _FADS1_ locus
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=47 "Edit section: FADS1 locus")]
Fatty acid desaturase 1 (FADS1) is a key enzyme in the metabolism of fatty acids.[[229]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-229) Moreover, rs174548 in the _FADS1_ gene shows increased correlation with DNA methylation in people with high abundance of CD8+ T cells.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221) SNP rs174548 is strongly associated with concentrations of arachidonic acid and other metabolites in fatty acid metabolism,[[230]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-230)[[231]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-pmid24816252-231) blood eosinophil counts.[[232]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-232) and inflammatory diseases such as asthma.[[233]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-233) Interaction results indicated a correlation between rs174548 and asthma, providing new insights about fatty acid metabolism in CD8+ T cells with immune phenotypes.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-Hawe_2022-221)
## Pseudoscience
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Epigenetics&action=edit&section=48 "Edit section: Pseudoscience")]
As epigenetics is in the early stages of development as a science and is surrounded by [sensationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensationalism "Sensationalism") in the public media, [David Gorski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Gorski "David Gorski") and geneticist [Adam Rutherford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Rutherford "Adam Rutherford") have advised caution against the proliferation of false and [pseudoscientific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience "Pseudoscience") conclusions by [new age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_age "New age") authors making unfounded suggestions that a person's genes and health can be manipulated by [mind control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brainwashing "Brainwashing"). Misuse of the scientific term by [quack authors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quackery "Quackery") has produced misinformation among the general public.[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-science-2)[[234]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigenetics#cite_note-234)
